# LicitaQui · Development Plan

> **Claims:** a card that exists because the product already promised something
> has a row in [`CLAIMS.md`](CLAIMS.md) as well — currently **D8**, **E5**, **E6**,
> **B8** and **D11b**. The card says what to build; the register says what was
> promised, where it renders and when it comes due. Close the row with evidence
> per recipient, never with a deploy.

> Version 0.3 · 2026-09-17 · companion to `TECHNICAL_SPEC.md` (v0.4) and the viability study (v10)
> Team: **one developer (Sci) + Claude Code agents**, 40+ h/week. Dates follow the Phase 0 schedule in the study.

---

## 1. Are we ready to start?

**Yes — development can start today.** Architecture, data model, UI, brand, prices and the core logic (PNCP collection, AI reading with answer keys) already exist. What is missing is a short list of decisions, accounts and one real product gap. None blocks day 1, but each blocks a specific milestone, so each has a due date.

## 2. How one person + Claude Code runs parallel work

1. **Contracts first.** Parallel agents only work if they share fixed contracts: the DB schema (migrations), the job table, and TypeScript types for API responses. Milestone M0 delivers these before any feature work.
2. **One stream = one git worktree = one Claude Code agent.** Each agent gets a task card (section 5) with inputs, acceptance criteria and the files it owns. Agents never edit files owned by another stream in the same cycle.
3. **Max 3 agents at a time.** The bottleneck is your review, not code generation. Queue the rest.
4. **Trunk-based, small PRs.** Every PR: CI green (lint, types, tests), preview deploy checked on a phone, merged the same day. No long-lived branches.
5. **Shared fixtures.** The 3 answer-key tenders (`gabaritos/`) plus 20 cached PNCP responses become the seed data for local dev, tests and previews, so web work never waits for the worker.
6. **You own:** decisions (section 1.2), reviews, production deploys, secrets, anything with money or legal text, and the non-dev track (influencers, interviews, concierge).

### Suggested `CLAUDE.md` for the repo

```markdown
# LicitaQui — agent guide
- Read docs/TECHNICAL_SPEC.md and docs/DEVELOPMENT_PLAN.md before any task. The task card is the scope; do not widen it.
- Code, identifiers, tables, commits: English. User-facing copy: Brazilian Portuguese in apps/web/messages/pt-BR.json.
- Never read PNCP/BrasilAPI/OpenRouter inside a web request; enqueue a job (spec §3).
- Never commit secrets. Use .env.example. Never log CPF, emails or tokens.
- Every change: tests for new logic, `pnpm lint && pnpm typecheck && pnpm test` (web) or `ruff && pytest` (worker) green.
- Schema changes only via db/migrations, in their own PR.
- AI prompt or extraction changes must run `worker/evaluation` and report the score diff.
- Design: use tokens from apps/web/styles/tokens.css (Ivory/Graphite/Blue, Archivo/IBM Plex). Brand name is always "LicitaQui".
```

## 3. Critical path and dependencies

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph M0["M0 · Foundation · 09-17→09-22"]
    A1[Repo + CI + envs] --> A2[Neon project + roles + branches]
    A2 --> A3[Migrations §6 + seed fixtures]
    A1 --> D1[Design tokens + base components]
  end
  subgraph M1["M1 · Offer live · 09-24"]
    D2[Offer page] --> F1[POST /api/founders + seats]
  end
  subgraph M2["M2 · Radar internal · 10-02"]
    B1[Worker skeleton + job queue] --> B2[sync_open_tenders]
    B2 --> B3[sync_items + segments]
    B2 --> B4[sync_files]
    B5[company_lookup BrasilAPI] --> B6[CNAE→segment map]
    B3 --> R1[Radar read API]
    B6 --> R1
    R1 --> D3[Landing + Radar + Opportunity screens]
    B4 --> C1[extract_text + ai_screening job]
    C1 --> D4[Screening screen]
  end
  subgraph M3["M3 · Founders opening · 10-08"]
    U1[Auth + visitor mode + quotas] --> E1[Telegram link + weekly digest]
  end
  subgraph M5["M5 · Billing live · 10-29"]
    P1[Asaas subscriptions + webhook] --> P2[promo_price_change job]
  end
  A3 --> D2
  A3 --> B1
  D1 --> D2
  D4 --> U1
  U1 --> P1
  G1((G1)) -.-> A2
  G3((G3)) -.-> F1
  G6((G6)) -.-> B6
```

**Critical path (anything late here moves 10-08):** A1 → A2 → A3 → B1 → B2 → B3 + B6 → R1 → D3 → C1 → D4 → U1 → E1.

**Safe to run in parallel with the critical path:** design tokens/components (D1), Offer page (D2/F1), BrasilAPI + CNAE map (B5/B6), AI job port (C1, against fixtures), email/Telegram texts (G10), `/admin` events (O1), and — from October — the awards backfill (B8), which needs weeks of collection before the Essencial price bands are meaningful.

### 4.1 Next actions — revisited 2026-10-09

**Eight days to the founders opening (Sat 17/10, 12:00 BRT — `docs/product.json`),
and that date carries two things at once:** the opening notice *and* Essencial
going on sale. Ordered by consequence, not by size.

| # | Next | Owner | Why it is next | Unblocks |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **G12** — Asaas production: company data, bank account, webhook URL, production key, **and four environment variables Sci must type** | **Sci** | The only item on the board whose duration we do not control. §1.2 dates it **10-20**, three days *after* the product starts charging. **The four variables are the half that was written down nowhere.** An agent cannot write `.env*` here — the permission layer refuses, the same deny rule that left `S3_BUCKET` and the AWS keys undocumented (see B4B's `STATUS.md` row) — so these are Sci's lines and they were living in a code comment that asserted G12 carried them when G12 did not: `ASAAS_API_KEY` (`$aact_prod_…`), `ASAAS_WEBHOOK_TOKEN` (the value set when the webhook is registered), `ASAAS_ENV=production`, and **`ASAAS_BILLING=live`** — which is the kill switch, defaults to a dry run on any other value *including unset*, and is therefore the one variable whose absence charges nobody and says nothing. On 17/10 at 12:00 BRT a deploy missing it serves every founder `billing.subscribe.error` with one `console.error` explaining why. **`ASAAS_ENV` and `ASAAS_API_KEY` must move together**: `configFault()` refuses a `$aact_prod_` key while `ASAAS_ENV` is anything but `production`, which is deliberate and is what stops a preview deployment charging real money | the production half of F2 |
| 2 | **The opening send** — re-queue the broadcast, land the rehearsal, alarm E20 | agent | Hours of work, and the only item whose failure mode is **nothing happening on the day while every signal stays green**. The sender is registered and correctly dated (`product.py:65` is `date(2026, 10, 17)`), but it is **not in `DEFAULT_SCHEDULE`** — its only production caller is `worker/scripts/schedule_founders_opening.py`, the queued row was deleted on 2026-10-03 (**E20**), and the rehearsal `c882236` (+1 039 lines, `task/e5-founders-opening`) is still **not an ancestor of `origin/main`**. `CLAIMS.md:60` still offers the deleted job as live evidence | **E5**, **E6**, **E12**, **E20** |

Original card:~~ **The founders e-mail needs content and a caller, not a transport — the transport already works.** Sci, 2026-09-24: *"I already received an email when I create an account."* He is right, and this card's first version was wrong to say there was no sender. `apps/web/lib/auth/magic-link-email.ts` talks to **Resend's REST API directly** from the web app (Auth.js's `sendVerificationRequest` hook), the sending domain is live, and the magic link arrives. What misled me is that the *worker* has no mail transport beside `whatsapp.py` and `telegram.py`, and `worker/templates/email/founders-*.md` sit next to the WhatsApp templates as though the worker were meant to send them — it is not, and nothing renders them. **So the work is: the founders e-mail content, and a decision about where it is sent from.** Two options with different costs: send from the **web app** at signup time, reusing the Resend call the magic link already makes (simplest, but it happens inside the request and a Resend failure is then in the signup's path); or give the **worker** a mail transport and enqueue it beside `send_whatsapp` (matches §3's rule that a web request never calls a third party, and inherits the `*.sent` event discipline E4 established). | E9 | E | ~~**Done 2026-09-29** — shipped in #151 and #152; the copy half closed in this PR (`radar.price.intro` drops *"o preço de mercado"*, which `plan_limits` grants to `pro` alone behind a permanent lock, so it was shown to nobody; `radar.landing.opportunity.body` and `plans.locked.priceBand` now carry the *"nos editais em que já temos dados de vencedores"* condition Sci approved on 28/09 for the other two sites). Original card:~~ **Phase 1 of the price band: build it, gate it on confidence, and show nothing where the data cannot carry it.** Sci's decision, 2026-09-28: *"This promise is the main goal of the application, and we have the data… only show the information when we have more 90% of sure… Im ok for items we dont have the correct information."* So the promise stays and becomes **conditional at the item level** — it is true wherever it appears because it only appears where it is backed.

**Measured before designing it** (2026-09-28, 200 random items from open editais): comparables are found by segment + unit + description similarity, and the funnel is `segment only 57 429 pairs → + unit 4 065 → + similarity ≥0.3 186`. Two findings. **`unit` is not normalised** — `Unidade`, `UNIDADE`, `UN`, `UND`, `unidade` are the same unit and match none of each other, which alone discards 93% of candidates; normalising is inside this card. **Product identity is the real constraint, not award volume**: after normalising, **7% of items reach ≥3 comparables, 5% reach ≥5, and 1% pass ≥5 with a tight spread**. Two councils buying the same printer describe it completely differently, and `ncm` — which would settle it — is **7.4% populated**. Raising that is **C3**, not this card.

**So phase 1 ships mostly blank and that is the point.** ~1% of items show a band today, and the figure rises on its own as `sync_awards` accumulates (931 tenders so far, days not months). What must not happen is a band shown where it is not earned. **The gate is a stated rule, not a claimed probability** — see C4: "90% sure" cannot be asserted without a back-test, and asserting it would be the exact defect `CLAIMS.md` collects. Until C4 measures it, the rule is spelled out (≥ N comparables, inter-quartile spread within X of the median, awards no older than Y) and the screen says what it is rather than naming a confidence.

~~**The 08/10 half of B8: `timeline.steps[3]` promises the price math on opening day.**~~ The original diagnosis still holds and is the rest of the work: *"Saiba quanto pagar para manter sua margem"*, added 2026-09-25. | H3 | H | ~~**Done 2026-10-01 — by removing Neon from CI entirely, which was the better answer.** Both workflows now run `postgres:18` as a service container (`db/ci_databases.py` creates and migrates every database from `db/migrations` on each run), so no CI job touches the production endpoint. Measured on the same suite, same tests: `integration` **38 min → 1 min**, `ci-web` ~3 min → **1 min**. The workflow's own diagnosis was right — *"1.8 s each against Neon in sa-east-1 where a round trip is ~200 ms"* — it was never the tests. It also **restores safety that had been traded away**: integration stopped running on pull requests because it was slow and expensive, and now runs on every PR for zero Neon compute. **The 16 databases are still on Neon and are no longer read by anything** — see B31 for dropping them.~~ Original card: **The 15 test databases share production's compute, so CI wakes the live endpoint.** Verified 2026-09-27: all 18 DSNs resolve to the single Neon endpoint `ep-flat-firefly-acai77cp` — `neondb` and `licitaqui_test*` alike, one branch, one compute. **This is the exact topology `TECHNICAL_SPEC.md:181` rejected** ("same compute; the other app can never scale to zero while LicitaQui runs — ❌"); the test databases are that other app. It cost nothing while the idle poll kept the compute awake 24/7, and it **started costing the day that was fixed** (#134): each integration run now wakes production's endpoint for ~28 minutes that it would otherwise have slept through. At ~22 runs/week after the PR→nightly change that is roughly **2.5 CU-hrs/week**. The fix is a Neon branch for CI — copy-on-write, so storage is nearly free — but it means recreating 15 databases and re-entering **16 GitHub secrets**, each a chance to break CI in a way that looks like a test failure. **Deliberately deferred past 08/10** on that risk, not on the merit. Also a real coupling worth naming: a hung or runaway test competes with the live site for one compute **Costed 2026-09-30, and it is no longer only tidiness.** The compute those 17 databases share is billed at **6,96 CU-h/day** and is awake **61% of the day** (B28). Every CI run therefore wakes **production's** endpoint and holds it for the run plus a **five-minute tail that cannot be shortened** — 300 s is the floor on Launch, verified by API refusal at 60/90/120/180/240 s. B22 made this worse on purpose and correctly: the 12 web database suites now run on **every pull request**, so the number of CI-driven wakes on the production endpoint went up by design. Separating them is now a cost change with a number attached, not housekeeping. Launch allows 100 projects, so a dedicated test project is available; it needs its own DSNs in the GitHub secrets and in `.env.local`, which is Sci's to do. **Prepared 2026-09-30: `worker/scripts/move_test_databases.py`.** Plan-only by default; `--create-project` makes a `licitaqui-tests` project in `aws-sa-east-1` with its own 300 s suspend, `--project-id <id> --commit` creates the databases in it. It **refuses to run against the production project id** and **never touches the existing databases** — a bad DSN is then a red CI run against an empty database, not lost fixtures. Dropping the originals is a separate, later act, once CI has been green against the new project for a few days. The DSNs are written to `.env.test-project.local` (gitignored) rather than printed: twenty-one connection strings in terminal scrollback is how they reach a paste buffer or a screenshot. **The list is read from `pg_database`, not written down**, and that is deliberate, because the names have already drifted: **21** `TEST_DATABASE_URL*` variables are read by code, CI sets **15**, and there are **16** databases. `D4`, `EV`, `IG`, `PNCP` and `R2` have no database of their own and silently fall back to `TEST_DATABASE_URL`; `U1` has a database and no CI secret. A constant here would have been wrong the first time somebody added a suite, and wrong silently — which is B25's shape. **Left to Sci**: creating the project, filling the DSNs and setting the secrets. | — | ∥ | 1 | `licitaqui_test*` resolve to a different `ep-` than `neondb`; a full CI run leaves production's endpoint suspended throughout; the 16 secrets are rotated in one sitting and a deliberate PR proves the suite still runs |
| H4 | H | **Nothing deletes the Neon branch a Vercel preview creates.** `TECHNICAL_SPEC.md:193` specifies it — *"cleanup follows Vercel's deployment retention (can take months) … add a GitHub Action that deletes the branch when the PR closes, or disable per-deployment branching"* — and it was never built: `.github/workflows/` has six files and none mentions a Neon branch. 134 PRs have closed since. **Whether this is costing anything is unverified**, and that is the first task: the Neon console's Branches list says in one screen whether orphans have piled up, and Free allows ten. If they have, the choice is the cleanup action or turning per-deployment branching off in the Vercel integration and using one shared `preview` branch, which is what `migrate.yml:5-7` assumes anyway ("Neon preview branches are not wired up yet"). | E14 | E | **`queue.enqueue` is the one real per-row write loop left, and it is not urgent.** Audited 2026-09-27 while investigating the Neon bill. Everything else is already batched: `tenders`, `tender_items`, `tender_files` and `awards` all go through `cur.executemany`, and `tenders.py:399` even harvests `returning=True` through `nextset()` — the docstring puts it at ~4 vs ~250 tenders/s against Neon sa-east-1. `ai_analyses` has nothing to batch at all, because one job is one analysis by design. **`queue.enqueue` (`queue.py:82`) is one INSERT + one `fetchone` per job**, and five sweeps call it in a loop: `sync_tenders._enqueue_followups` (up to **400** per 200-tender flush, twice per changed tender), `tender_value.enqueue_refreshes` (**400** per half-hourly sweep), `title_tender.enqueue_pending` (**500** hourly), `sync_awards` (200 nightly), `telegram_alerts.weekly_digest` (one per user). **Explicitly not a cost fix** — the compute runs at 0.02 of 0.25 vCPU, so round trips are not what the bill is made of; this is throughput, and it is carded so the finding is not lost rather than because it is due. The constraint on any rewrite: the partial unique index means `on conflict (kind, key) where status in ('queued','running') do nothing`, and **every caller counts which rows were rejected**, so a multi-row version must `returning kind, key` and the caller must diff against its input rather than count rows. Copy `tenders.py:399`'s `executemany(..., returning=True)` + `nextset()` loop rather than inventing a placeholder builder; the repo has no `unnest` insert helper and no `COPY` anywhere. **Do not start this without a measurement attached** | — | ∥ | 0.5 | A sweep that enqueues 500 jobs issues a bounded number of statements instead of 500; the dedupe count each caller reports is identical to today's, proven against the real database; `sweep_titles` wall time measured before and after |

| D19 | D | ~~**The Radar header says "sem CNAE lido" while the list beside it groups 62 editais by that company's CNAE.**~~ **Done 2026-10-06 — and the card's first cause had moved, so read this before trusting the diagnosis below.** The fix is one field: `GET /api/radar/tenders` now answers with `groupedBy` — the CNPJ it resolved, the company it read for it and how many **CNAEs** that company has — and `RadarView` takes a single `grouping` prop in place of `company`, so the header, its CNAE count and the tab help are all functions of the one field the route filled. **Correction to cause (1):** `app/radar/page.tsx`'s literal `company={null}` is the **Suspense fallback** today, not the live screen — it renders for one server frame before `RadarScreen` mounts, and `CompanyLine` now reads that frame as *unknown* rather than *no company*. The live defect was entirely in `radar-screen.tsx`, which resolved the company only inside `if (cnpj)` — i.e. only when the URL carried `?cnpj=` — while the route resolved `params.cnpj ?? visitor.cnpj`. The visitor cookie is `httpOnly`, so the browser could not have fixed this by looking harder: it had to be told. **`cnaeCount` now counts CNAEs** (`main_cnae` plus `secondary_cnaes`, deduplicated, in `readCompany`'s own query) instead of `company.segments.length`, which counts the 14 POC-1 segments those CNAEs reach — different numbers in both directions, since one CNAE can reach three segments and B6 leaves 777 codes reaching none. **Two strings are drafts awaiting Sci** (legal brief §5): `radar.list.noCompany` *"Sem empresa informada"* and `radar.list.groupHintNoCnae` *"sem CNAE lido para comparar"*, the second replacing *"seu CNAE atende"* / *"pode haver exigências"* on the two tabs that assert a CNAE match when none was read. The grouping itself was untouched, as the card asked. Tests: `app/radar/radar-header.test.tsx` holds the header and the tab help against each other across four states × three tabs; `e2e/journeys/radar-header.spec.ts` drives the cookie path a unit test cannot reach (no `?cnpj=`, CNPJ behind the cookie); `radar.db.test.ts` proves the route reports the cookie CNPJ and that a company-less keyword search groups everything as `keyword`. Both were run against the old behaviour and failed — six of seven unit assertions, and the cookie journey — before being run against the new one. **An independent review of the diff found three defects the green suite did not** (CLAUDE.md §4b), all fixed before the PR: the first fix passed `grouping?.cnaeCount ?? 0` to the tab help, which collapses *unknown* into *zero* — so while the header carefully withheld, the hint asserted *"sem CNAE lido para comparar"* on the server frame, for the whole of `waitForData`'s 60-second window and on every timeout, and the invariant test watched it go past because it forbade two named pairs instead of requiring agreement; a `sessionStorage` snapshot written by the **previous build** passed `valid()` and restored a grouped list with no grouping, re-creating this very defect at deploy time with the company's name lost; and the database assertion on `cnaeCount` could not fail under the old implementation, because `soleCnaeFor` builds a fixture whose CNAE and segment counts happen to be equal. `GroupedBy.cnpj` was also **removed**: nothing read it, and it was the one place an `httpOnly` cookie's CNPJ reached page JavaScript and `sessionStorage` (§12). **Follow-ups carded, not fixed here:** **D53** — `manualCnae` and `noSegments` are still decided from the `?cnpj=` company alone; **D55** — bare `/radar` still refuses the cookie CNPJ the route would have resolved, which is the entry point nearly every link uses; **D54** — a db test whose assertions read rows the suite does not own. The two draft strings have a row in `docs/TO_VALIDATE.md` (#13). Original card:~~ **The Radar header says "sem CNAE lido" while the list beside it groups 62 editais by that company's CNAE.** Found by Sci, 2026-09-28, on `/radar?q=saas`: *"I didnt enter my CNPJ, so how those tenders can be compatible with a null CNPJ?"* — the right question. Two causes, and they compound. **(1) `app/radar/page.tsx:37` passes `company={null}` as a literal**, so `CompanyLine` computes `cnaes = company ? company.segments.length : 0` → 0 → *"sem CNAE lido"* **always**, whatever the visitor actually has. **(2) The list does not use that null**: `GET /api/radar/tenders` resolves the CNPJ as `params.cnpj ?? visitor.cnpj` — **from the visitor cookie** — then reads the company, splits its segments with `segmentsByFit` and groups on them. So the header and the list answer from different sources and the header is the one that is wrong. **Why it is a claim and not only a bug:** the selected tab renders `radar.list.tabHelp.compatible` = *"seu CNAE atende"* directly beneath a line saying no CNAE was read. A user cannot tell which to believe, and the one that is false is the one asserting a match — the same shape `CLAIMS.md` exists for. Note the counter is also mislabelled independently of this: `cnaeCount` renders *"N CNAEs"* but counts **segments**, which are derived from the CNAE and are a different number. **Not a grouping bug** — `labels([])` correctly yields `array[]::text[]` and `&&` against it is false, so a genuinely company-less visitor groups everything as `keyword`; that path is right and should stay. Decide also what the header should say when a cookie CNPJ is driving the list but no company has been read yet, which is a real state and currently renders as a confident falsehood | — | ∥ | 0.5 | The header reports the company the list is actually grouping by, from one source; with no company at all it says so and every tender lands in `keyword`; with a cookie CNPJ it names that company; `cnaeCount` counts what its string says it counts, or the string changes; a test renders the header and the tab help together and fails if one claims a CNAE match while the other reports none |

| C3 | C | **A canonical product key per item, because free text is why the band is blank.** E9 measured the constraint: same-segment candidates are plentiful (~574 per item) and description similarity collapses them to ~2, so **1% of open items can be priced**. The cause is that PNCP item descriptions are written by each órgão in its own words — *"CADEIRA GIRATORIA COM BRACOS"* and *"Cadeira de escritório giratória, com apoio de braço"* are the same product and share almost no trigrams. `ncm` would settle it and is **7.4% populated** (31 367 of 421 202 items), so it cannot be the key. **The fix is extraction, the same shape as `short_title`**: one AI call per item producing a normalised product key — noun phrase, material, capacity, unit — stored beside the item and matched exactly. **The cost is the decision, not the code**: 421 202 items at the ~R$ 0.0001 per call `title_tender` measures is ~R$ 42 one-off, but the throughput matters more than the money — `sweep_titles` drains 500/hour, so the full corpus is **35 days** at that cadence, and a dedicated sweep needs its own rate and its own budget line. **Measure on a sample first** and record the number before the full run — B18's card already requires that discipline and B17's did not, which is why this says so. Start with the items that can pay for themselves: awarded items (6 094) and items on currently-open editais, not the whole history **2026-09-30: C3 is now load-bearing for the band existing at all, not for it being good.** The trigram matcher was pricing a cast-iron desk punch from cadernos and a *compatível* toner from *original* cartridges — measured over 103 open items, the median share of comparables that were even the same product was **0%**. `lib/radar/product-key.ts` now requires the same product and the same grade, which is correct and costs nearly all the remaining coverage: over one 400-item sample across every segment the items keeping any comparable fell **62 → 14**, and the bands were **0 → 0**, because a band is already rare enough that 400 items find none either way. A canonical product key is what brings the number back; until it exists the feature is true and almost never renders. | E9 | ∥ | 2 | A sample of ≥ 500 items is keyed, the cost per item and the projected total are recorded in `docs/STATUS.md` **before** any full run; two items a person judges to be the same product receive the same key, asserted on a hand-built set; the share of open items reaching ≥ 5 comparables is re-measured against E9's 5% baseline and the improvement stated as a number; no full-corpus run happens without that number in front of Sci |
| C4 | C | **Measure the band's accuracy before any screen claims one.** Sci asked for *"more 90% of sure"*; that number cannot currently be produced, only asserted — there is no model and no back-test, and asserting it would be precisely the defect `CLAIMS.md` exists to collect. **It is measurable, and cheaply**, because the answer is already in the table: take editais that have **already closed and been awarded**, compute the band from awards that existed **before** that award date, and compare the prediction to what actually won. That is a real hit rate — *"the winning price fell inside the band in N% of cases"* — over a held-out set rather than a number chosen because it sounds confident. **Design notes that decide whether the figure means anything:** the comparable set must exclude the tender being predicted and anything awarded after it, or the measurement leaks the answer into itself; report by segment, because 6 segments carry awards and 5 carry none, and a national average would hide that; and report coverage beside accuracy, since a rule that fires on 1% of items and is right every time is a different product from one that fires on 40% and is right four times in five. Only once this exists may a percentage appear on screen, and the screen must say what it is a percentage **of** **2026-10-03 — B35 stores two columns for this card and C4's text never mentioned them.** `catalog_prices.supply_unit` and `catalog_prices.catalog_item_code` are written by the ingest, read by no screen, and justified in the migration as *"C4's"* — the offline re-tuning that storing rows rather than band summaries exists to allow. Named here so the handoff is a card rather than a comment: `supply_unit` is how *"the PDM mixes units"* can be told from *"the market disagrees"* when re-tuning `MAX_SPREAD`, and `catalog_item_code` makes the CATMAT regrouping repeatable at zero API cost. **It must not become a band key**: measured 2026-10-03, a CATMAT-level band gives 2.7× the coverage at a 33% hit rate, p<0.001 below the ~50% a correct quartile band scores, against PDM-level's 47%. | E9 | ∥ | 1 | A back-test over closed awarded editais reports hit rate and coverage per segment, with the comparable set provably excluding the target tender and any award dated after it — asserted by a test, not by inspection; the figure is written into `docs/STATUS.md` with its date and sample size; `docs/CLAIMS.md` gains a row for any percentage that reaches a user-facing screen; and E9's gate thresholds are re-tuned against the result rather than left at their initial guess |

Items-covered-per-code and bands-found-per-code are not the same objective, and this card optimises the first. **C4** is measuring whether the threshold rather than the ordering is what should change. **There is no bulk endpoint** — `tipo`+`codigo` are mandatory and a date-window-only request is a 404 — so the cost is intrinsically per code. | E22 | E | **The screen holds three kinds of evidence and shows one, so 10 of every 11 items that have a past winner say *"ainda sem dados"*.** Measured 2026-10-01, after the awards backfill took the priced corpus from 5 408 to **23 448** items: of 600 open items, **11.17% have at least one past winner of the same product**, 4.83% have two or more, 3.33% have three or more — and **0.67% show a band**, because `priceBand` returns `null` below `MIN_SAMPLE = 5` and the count is thrown away. The screen cannot tell *"one winner"* from *"nothing"*, so it says nothing to **94% of the items it has evidence for**. **The ladder** (Sci, 2026-10-01): ≥5 editais with a tight spread → the band and the preço-alvo, unchanged; 2–4 → the count and the individual results, never called a band and never a target price; exactly 1 → that single result; 0 → the sentence that explains the bar. **The thin rungs must show *what* was matched**, and that is not decoration: `MIN_SAMPLE` and `MAX_SPREAD` were what caught a wrong match — five editais outvote one caderno — and at a single edital **nothing** checks the product-identity heuristic, so a bad match is shown unopposed and looks more authoritative for being one clean number. The top rung earns the right to hide its sources because the spread did the judging; the thin rungs borrow the reader's judgement instead, by printing the matched description beside the price. **Free taste, Sci's ruling 2026-10-01, and the line is raw evidence free / computation paid**: the matched past results — count, descriptions, individual values — are free at every rung, and the **band (quartiles) and the preço-alvo stay Essencial**. Drawn that way the ladder is monotonic; *"free below 5, locked at 5"* would show a non-subscriber **less** at four editais than at three, which is the inversion this wording avoids. `locked` therefore carries the evidence too. Copy approved by Sci: *"Mostramos a faixa quando encontramos pelo menos 5 editais encerrados com o mesmo produto. Neste item encontramos N."* — ICU plural, wording his to adjust in place. **No `{low, high}` range, decided 2026-10-01 after review of the data layer**, for three reasons that arrived together: its extremes could be *invisible* (`samples` is capped at 4 and sorted newest-first, and an undated award sorts last, so the row driving the low was routinely the one the cap dropped — a reader was shown *"R$ 1,00 – R$ 210,00 · 5 editais"* with the R$ 1,00 absent from every result under it); it inherited **none** of `MAX_SPREAD`, so the caderno-beside-notebook mismatch that `priceBand` refuses outright got drawn as a span anyway; and together with the four sampled values it **reconstructed the paid band exactly** (verified: 5 editais, oldest cheapest → `samples ∪ range` is the whole per-edital set, and `priceBand` over it returns the real low/median/high to the cent). The printed results carry the span instead, where every number has an edital and a description beside it. **Open, Sci's call (see `TO_VALIDATE.md`): what the locked rung may send.** Even without the range, four raw per-edital values at ≥5 editais determine two of the band's three figures and bracket the third, so *raw evidence free* and *the band is paid* cannot both hold at the top rung. The two coherent answers are to accept that the band is effectively free wherever it exists (and sell the preço-alvo and the margin calculator, which stay genuinely unreconstructible), or to send the count and the descriptions but not the values once the band exists — an inversion, but one confined to the **0.67%** of items that reach it, and in the ordinary paywall direction | E9 | ∥ | 1 | A thin rung renders the matched description beside every value, and a test proves a single-edital item never renders a range, a median or a preço-alvo; the band and the preço-alvo are absent for a plan without the entitlement while the evidence is present, asserted for both an entitled and an unentitled caller; no rung shows a non-subscriber less than a weaker rung would; `CLAIMS.md` row 85 is updated, because the claim becomes three claims |

The **AI notice moved to the foot of the reading** at Sci's instruction (*"the disclaimer is [necessary], can be at the footer of this page"*); the test that pinned it above the button is rewritten to pin what still holds. **One thing to confirm, and it is Sci's**: legal brief §2.2 rule 5 says the notice appears on every result screen, and it does — but it no longer sits beside the action, and the action is now always on screen, so a reader can press it without having scrolled to the sentence. Whether the rule also means *before acting* has not been answered. The cost line (`screeningCost` / `screeningCostReady`) followed the action into the bar rather than being deleted with the button it lived under — it is one of the five strings this repo names as its own defect and it is not going back to rendering nowhere; the bar shows it when the next press spends a triagem and the remaining count when it does not. The count is still passed on the edital screen alone, because the triagem and preço primaries spend no triagem. **Still open, and restored here because #198 deleted the whole sentence when it had fixed only half of it** (the B26/B27 lesson, one PR later): the bar is a bare `<div>` **outside every landmark** — its ancestry is `div → div → div → body` — and now that the in-page button is gone it is the screen's *only* call to action, so for a landmark-navigating reader the primary action is in no region at all, at every width. That is worse than before, not better. A labelled `<nav>` fixes it and **the label is a sentence only Sci can approve**; the alternative, moving the bar inside `<main>`, would cap it at `max-w-[960px]` and change a layout that is measured and correct. Also unrecorded until now: the bar is in the flow, so it permanently takes **92.5px** of every desktop viewport, and a page that previously fit can now scroll. **Two more the review found and #198 did not change, named rather than silently left:** the triagem screen still has *two* links to the preço screen — the `LockedBlock` and the bar's primary — which is what Sci asked for (*"could be like ad"*: the block sells, the bar acts) and is not the strict-mode duplication the edital screen had, because the accessible names differ; and on the preço screen `showPlanCta === false` hides the whole bar including its second slot, so an entitled subscriber's only way back is the AppBar's *"Voltar"*, which on that screen goes to the triagem and not to the edital. **(2) is PARKED, 2026-10-02, by measurement** — and its blocker is **C3**, not F5 (F5 shipped). The card's own condition is *"rendered only where a band exists for at least one item"*, and answering that has no cheap form. Measured on three real 60-item open editais: asking every item costs **51 495 ms / 34 382 ms / 30 210 ms** — on the most-visited screen in the product — and **0 of those 180 items had a band**. B21's independent figure is 412 ms median, 2 715 ms worst per item. A batched query (the SQL half of `comparablesForItem` grouped by item) costs **315–1 005 ms** and cleared two of the three editais outright, which is sound because the TS product gate only ever *removes* rows, so a count under `MIN_SAMPLE` there is a definitive no; but on the third it narrowed 60 items to 17 worth asking about, of which the real path found **0**, and asking those properly would still cost ~8 s. So the work as carded ships a block that costs seconds and, on measured data, renders for nobody — this repo's named defect, one shade louder. **D33** says the same of the same signal: a precomputed per-item flag written by a job, *"worth nothing until C3"*. Unpark when C3 lands and coverage is re-measured; `docs/PRICE_BAND.md` §6 holds the numbers. It still also needs a `CLAIMS.md` sentence from Sci, since it puts a price on a screen that never carried one. **Agreed order, 2026-09-30:** D30 → D25 (3)+(4) → F5 → D25 (2) → D26 → D27, with **D27 as the cut line** if 08/10 presses: the preço-alvo block is the product's proposition and is reachable today only by spending a triagem, while the two-state block is a retention story for people who have already paid. D26 is Python and D25 is TypeScript and they share no files, so they parallelise — **provided D26's `contract.ts` type is defined first**, which is what stops D27 waiting two days for the worker. Original card: **The decision is below the fold, and the number that drives it costs a triagem to reach.** Sci's journey, 2026-09-29, and canvas 11 (`11 · Radar mobile`). Five parts, one piece, because they are the same reading order and three of them land in `opportunity-view.tsx`. **(1) Objeto trimmed with a disclosure.** Sci's ruling 2026-09-29: keep it where it is, 3–4 lines, "ler tudo". This preserves two earlier rulings and **reverses a third**: position (*"The Objeto should be right after the box with the price"*) and full width (2026-09-22, *"why does the Objeto text not take the entire width like the table above?"*) both stand; `renders expanded, with no "ler mais" toggle to pay for` does not. **Invert that test, never delete it** — a deleted guard on this component is how the block's position was silently reverted once already — and note its assertion is a literal `/ler mais/i`, so "ler tudo" would pass a guard written to stop exactly this. Use `trimObject` (real truncation, the 0.6 word-boundary rule), **not** `line-clamp`, which the same test forbids and which leaves the whole string in the accessibility tree. **(2) The preço-alvo block on the Edital screen**, unlocked where the plan includes the band and locked-with-shape where it does not (`LockedBlock` + `LockedValue` already draw it; `price-view.tsx` already renders that exact shape). **Rendered only where a band exists for at least one item** — measured coverage is ~1% of open items, and a lock over an empty box converts a founder into a refund. **This is a new user-facing promise on a screen that never carried a price: it needs its `docs/CLAIMS.md` row in the same PR.** It is also the expensive half — `opportunity-screen.tsx` never calls `getBand` and `page.tsx` reads `readPriceBandEntitlement()` only inside `PricePane`, so this adds a trigram join to the most-visited screen in the product. **(3) A fixed bottom action bar, `lg:hidden`.** `sheet.tsx` already reserves `z-40` for it below the drawer's `z-50`, and the whole app has one other `z-`. Two slots, primary = the next action with the verb changing by state. Every tender screen's `main` is `pb-10`, too little for a ~72px bar, so all three need the padding; the items list is what will clip. At `lg` the rail is **in the layout flow** with a width that is localStorage state (56/264px), so no fixed offset is correct — the bar does not render there and the CTA stays where it is. **(4) The remaining triagem count as a caption under the button, never inside its label** — a primary action that is also a status readout resizes under itself and cannot be translated. `AccountSummary.screenings` is already a full `QuotaView` read server-side once per route by `app/radar/layout.tsx`; `AppMenuContext` publishes `{open}` alone, so this is ~5 lines. **Do not call `readShell()` again in `page.tsx`** — a session read outside the Suspense boundary costs the whole page on a cold Neon. The server fallback renders the bar without the count and fills in; that is correct, not a bug. **(5) The price screen**, Sci's journey note: drop *"Exemplo real (papel A4)"* (delete its keys too, or card them — a string nothing renders is this repo's named defect), item with a published winner first (`TenderItemView.hasAward` already exists, so the ordering is free — **and that was wrong, measured 2026-09-30: `has_award` is the item's *own* award, so it is `true` for 1 358 of 414 594 items on open editais (0.33%) and for **zero** items on every edital Sci has looked at. `chooseItem` therefore falls through to `items[0]` on every open tender, which is the defect this part was written to fix. See **D33**), and the `(1)(2)` pager becomes a vertical list — the chips are `min-h-8`, under the 44px `--spacing-touch`. **Names and status only: no per-item reference figures.** The band is fetched one item at a time because it costs a trigram join; an inline list of N items with figures is N round trips or a new endpoint, and neither is in this card. **Out of scope, named so this does not grow:** the two-state compatibility block and the meter (**D26**, **D27**), the copy fixes (**D28**), the 900px/1024px breakpoint (**D29**), and everything in the P3 list Sci has not yet validated **2026-10-03 — the measurement part (2) was waiting for exists, and it argues for staying parked.** Its blocker was never latency: *"what share of **editais** have at least one band"* was unmeasured. Now measured over 25 045 open editais with a mapped item: **26.6% have ≥1 band-eligible item**, and applying the per-code band rate gives **4.3%** showing an actual band (7.3% if the random-sample rate holds) — **roughly 1 edital in 23**. So a preço-alvo block on the most-visited screen in the product would be blank on 22 of every 23 screens. Phone and tablet behaviour is unchanged; only the case the viewport query could not see moves. The `@container` is on the block and **not** on `main`, deliberately — `container-type: inline-size` also makes an element a containing block for `fixed` descendants, and D25 proposes a fixed bottom bar, so putting it on `main` would have broken D25 later without anything saying so. **The unit test could not have caught this and a green suite would have shipped it**: `renderToStaticMarkup` has no layout, so `opportunity-view.test.tsx` can pin the *mechanism* (a container, a container query, no viewport query) and never the *result*. The widths are asserted in a browser instead — `e2e/journeys/tender-columns.spec.ts`, at 390, 1023, 1024, 1164, 1440 and 1024 again with the rail collapsed. Mutation-checked three ways, each mutation asserted to apply and each restore byte-identical by `md5`: reverting to the viewport query reddens **the two 1024px browser tests and only those** — the other four pass under both queries, because that is exactly where the two agree. The order guard's marker was **updated, never deleted**. Original card: **Between 1024px and ~1164px the tender's two-column block is drawn in 760px.** Found 2026-09-29 while reviewing canvas 11; nothing on the board caused it. The compatibility/Operação grid switches to two columns at `min-[900px]` — a **viewport** query — while the desktop rail appears at `lg` (1024px) and takes **264px** out of the content width (`app-shell.tsx`; the width is localStorage state, 56px collapsed or 264px). So the first 140px above `lg` render two columns inside roughly 760px of usable width, each column ~360px, on a block whose own comment sets a 40px column gap for a layout it assumes is wide. The two numbers were chosen in different cards and neither knew about the other: `min-[900px]` predates the rail, which D20 shipped on 2026-09-29. Either make it a container query, so the block responds to the space it is actually given rather than to the window, or move the breakpoint to `min-[1180px]`. The container query is the better answer and is the reason to prefer it over a magic number: the rail can also be **collapsed to 56px**, and a viewport query cannot see that either | — | ∥ | 0.25 | At every width from 900px to 1440px, with the rail expanded and collapsed, the block is two columns only where each column has room for its content; a test pins the rule so the next person changing either number cannot silently reintroduce the overlap |

| H2 | A/D | **The `Content-Security-Policy` shipped 2026-09-25 is deliberately partial — `script-src`/`style-src`/`connect-src`/`img-src` are unset, not restricted.** `next.config.ts`'s `SECURITY_HEADERS` closes the audit's clickjacking and MIME-sniffing findings (`X-Frame-Options`, `frame-ancestors`, `X-Content-Type-Options`, HSTS, `Permissions-Policy`) plus the CSP directives that cannot interact with a script (`object-src`, `base-uri`, `form-action`), but leaves script execution unrestricted because GTM (container GTM-5V6M75R7) can inject further inline scripts for whatever tags are configured inside it in Google's UI, which nothing in this repository enumerates — a strict `script-src` would allow or silently break those tags with no diff to review. This card is that follow-up: audit what is actually configured inside the GTM container, then either thread a nonce through `app/layout.tsx`'s bootstrap `<Script>` (GTM's documented mechanism) and into whatever GTM template needs one, or hash-allowlist the known-static scripts — verified against a real deploy with GTM's own preview mode, not just a local build, since a broken tag would not show up in `pnpm build` | H1 | ∥ | 0.5 | `script-src`, `style-src`, `connect-src` and `img-src` are each either explicit and non-empty or the card records why one stays open; GTM's preview mode confirms every currently-configured tag still fires against the new policy; the four directives H2 was blocked on either land or are named individually as still open, not folded into a generic "harden CSP further" |
| B8 | B | ~~`sync_awards` + awards backfill for concierge segments (starts collecting data for v1 price bands)~~ — **measured 2026-09-28 and the collecting half is done.** `awards` holds **6 094 rows** against an acceptance criterion of ≥ 5k; `sync_awards` is registered, scheduled daily at 03:00 BRT, and has run. **CPF masking verified on the real table**: all 6 094 `supplier_doc` values are 14 characters (CNPJ), the single `person_type = PF` row is masked, and **0 rows carry an 11-digit run anywhere in `raw`** — so no unmasked CPF survives in the payload either, which is the half of spec §12 that is easy to miss. **What looked like a defect and is not:** 328 of 1 259 `sync_tender_awards` jobs sit in `failed`, 322 of them `CircuitOpen: circuit 'pncp-resultados' is open`. That is a historical scar from a PNCP outage, not data loss — **261 of those 326 tenders (80%) already hold awards**, re-probed by a later sweep, and **0 award jobs have failed in the last 24 hours**. The design is why: `mark_probed` sits after the `try` and `CircuitOpen` re-raises, so a tender that dies on the breaker never gets its 168-hour cooldown marker, stays selected by `PENDING_TENDERS_SQL`, and resumes for free on the `not exists` predicate with every item already fetched kept. Recorded here so nobody re-investigates it: the `failed` rows are **queue noise, not lost editais**. The one real cost is that a real failure is now hard to see among 322 breaker failures — `BREAKER_RESET_SECONDS` is 900 while the first two backoffs are 120 + 480, so a job meeting an open circuit burns three of four attempts waiting on it. That is a queue-wide property, not a B8 one, and it belongs to whoever next touches `consumer.py`. **What is left of this card is not in this card**: the winning-band presentation and the framing rules below are **E9**, whose own text calls itself *"the 08/10 half of B8"*. | B11 | B | **`CircuitOpen` must not burn a job's attempts.** Observed 2026-09-23: 59 `sync_tender_awards` jobs `failed` with `CircuitOpen: circuit 'pncp-resultados' is open` — dequeued, failed and charged one of their four attempts each **without a request ever leaving the process**. The breaker raises *before* the call, so `attempts` records an attempt that never happened, and a long enough PNCP outage exhausts a job permanently for a reason that has nothing to do with that job — the same shape as the 404 bug in #54: a transient condition made terminal. Fix in the consumer's error handling, not the claim statement: treat `CircuitOpen` as a **deferral** — re-queue without incrementing `attempts`, and set `run_after` from **the breaker's own reset clock** (the health endpoint already reports `retry_in_s`), not the generic 2/8/30 ladder. The `run_after` half is not optional: skipping only the increment leaves `next_backoff(attempts)` stuck on its first tier, so the job wakes every 2 minutes for the whole outage — ~90 pointless `UPDATE`s over three hours instead of ~12, which §14.1 says to design out. **Not** deferring the dequeue by kind: a kind→breaker map does not exist and is not clean (`sync_items` reads `pncp-itens` *and* reaches `pncp-consulta` via `absence.py`), and refusing to dequeue blocks work that would have succeeded — circuits are per-endpoint, job kinds are not. **Caveat to build in:** free deferral trades a visible wrong state for an invisible one, so record the deferral count and fail the job after a generous threshold (many hours of continuous deferral, not 4 attempts). The goal is "PNCP's weather must not kill a job", not "never give up" | B2 | ∥ | 0.5 | A job deferred by an open circuit keeps its `attempts`; its `run_after` matches the breaker's reset rather than the ladder; a three-hour simulated outage produces ~12 wake-ups, not ~90; a job deferred past the threshold fails visibly with the deferral count in `jobs.error` |
| B12 | B | **Breaker policy: count failures in a window, not consecutively** (ADR-0001 re-measurement). Measured 2026-09-22 over 50 minute-spaced probes of `/api/consulta`: **31×200, 3×502, 1×503, 15×timeout — 62% success, failing in runs of two to four minutes**. The ADR describes PNCP as *down* or *degraded*; neither fits. The run structure is the load-bearing part, not the percentage: a consecutive-failure breaker trips on a run of two, so a service that is **mostly up** trips it repeatedly, and a 15-minute reset means one bad minute costs fifteen good ones. On 2026-09-23 at 07:48 that had five of seven breakers open or half-open against endpoints answering most requests, stalling `sync_items` and `sync_files` for hours. The consequence worth stating plainly: **a 62%-success service can be less available to us than a 40%-success one**, if the first fails in runs and the second fails independently — which two-mode framing structurally cannot express. Evaluate a windowed policy (failures per N requests or per T seconds) against the recorded probe data before changing production, and add *flapping* to ADR-0001 as a third failure mode with its own response | B11 | | 1 | The recorded 50-probe trace is replayed against both policies and the window policy keeps the endpoint usable where the consecutive policy does not; ADR-0001 documents the third mode; no change ships without that replay |
| B13 | B | **`confidential_budget` must be able to say "we do not know".** The column is `boolean default false`, but `tenders._confidential()` deliberately returns `None` when PNCP sent neither `orcamentoSigilosoCodigo` nor `orcamentoSigiloso` — its own comment reads *"absence of evidence is not 'public'"*. **The schema collapses unknown into not-confidential before anything reads it**, so no screen can tell the two apart however carefully it is written. ~~Every search-sourced row is affected: measured 2026-09-23, **108 tenders hold `estimated_value = 0` and 0 of them have `confidential_budget = true`**~~ — **that measurement expired.** Re-measured 2026-09-28 against production: `NULL` **21 031**, `false` 7 417, `true` **514**; among rows with `estimated_value = 0`, 510 NULL / 172 false / **118 true**. Unknown is the majority value and `true` is written 514 times, so the collapse this card described is not happening — #65's consulta upgrade closed it and nobody re-measured. **NULL was also already allowed**, so "allow NULL" was never a change. | B47 | B | **A guard against a stale fixture date fails on every full local run, and CI cannot see it.** `tests/test_neon_usage.py::test_the_fixtures_are_dated_from_the_real_clock` asserts `abs(datetime.now(UTC) - NOW) < 300`, where `NOW = datetime.now(UTC).replace(microsecond=0)` is bound at **module import** (`:32`). Import happens at collection, the test executes near the end of the run, so the quantity it measures is **the suite's own elapsed time** — not whether `NOW` came from the clock, which is the thing it is written to prove. Measured on `main` @ `9957884` on 2026-10-09: it failed with `assert 2706.174844 < 300`, i.e. the full suite's 45:37. **It passes everywhere nobody needs it and fails where somebody does**: the 42-second fast suite, CI's unit job (`pytest` with no database secrets, so every integration test skips) and CI's integration job (81 s against a local Postgres) all sit far under 300 s, so `main`'s CI has been green throughout. Any full local run is 45 minutes and fails. B23's intent was right — a literal date must fail now rather than on the morning the window moves past it — but the assertion has to compare `NOW` against **import time**, not against the clock at execution time: bind `IMPORTED_AT = datetime.now(UTC)` beside it and assert the two agree within a second or two, which catches a literal date immediately and is indifferent to how long the suite takes. Worth fixing because of what it costs in judgement, not in minutes: a lane that runs the full suite sees a failure that is nothing to do with its work, in a file called `test_neon_usage`, and B44 is the card about a suite nobody can finish | — | ∥ | 0.25 | `NOW` is held to import time rather than to execution time; a literal date in that constant still fails the test; and a full local run of `pytest tests/` no longer fails on it |

## 6. Weekly lanes (what runs at the same time)

Max 3 agents in parallel; Sci reviews and owns S-tasks.

| Week | Agent 1 (critical path) | Agent 2 | Agent 3 | Sci |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 09-17 → 09-20 | A1 → A2 | D1 | E0 (welcome + opening texts) | G1 (create Neon via Vercel), G4, G5; start G3 |
| 09-21 → 09-27 | A3 → B1 | D2 → F1 → E2 | B0 → B5 | Approve Offer copy and welcome text; **Offer live 09-24**; connect Evolution API instance; outreach |
| 09-28 → 10-04 | B2 → B3 → R1 | B6 (CNAE map) | C1 (AI port) → D3 | Review CNAE map; interviews; approve G10 texts |
| 10-05 → 10-11 | D4 → U1 | E1 | O2 | **Founders opening 10-08**; choose 20 concierge |
| 10-12 → 10-18 | H1 | B8 (awards backfill) | C2 | **Public Landing 10-13**; 1st digest |

| D26 | D | **The two-state compatibility block needs a checklist that exists nowhere. This card builds the object; D27 draws it.** Canvas 11 shows *"3 de 4 conferidos"* before the triagem and *"5 de 7 conferidos"* after — the only artefact in the product that shows a user what their triagem bought, and the best answer to *"what did my R$ 75 buy"*. It cannot be drawn today. **Before:** `reasons()` in `opportunity-view.tsx` emits a variable-length `string[]` of positives derived from `matchedSegments` and PNCP fields — no per-row verdict, no denominator, no page, no *unknown* state. **After:** `ScreeningModel` already carries `qualification`/`requirements` as `Finding[]` with `page` and `pageUnverified`, plus `blockers`, `score` and `citations {citations, verified, rate}` — so the page references and the score are already produced, on the screening screen. **What is missing is the join**: "3 de 4" and "5 de 7" must be *the same checklist seen twice*, and no such object exists in the worker, the API or `contract.ts`. This card is data only, no UI. Three rules it must encode. **A fixed set of requirement keys** (CNAE, regime ME/EPP, prazo, atestado técnico, capital mínimo, amostra, consórcio, entrega/local…), so the denominator is defensible rather than invented per tender. **Three states, not two** — `ok`, `blocker`, and an explicit `unknown` for what neither source can answer; the board's own prose concedes the third (*"o que daqui não dá para saber: sua regularidade fiscal e o que a comissão vai decidir"*), and a two-colour meter buries it. **Provenance per row** — `pncp` or `ai`, because a fact read from a structured field and a finding read out of a PDF with a page citation are not the same kind of true, and one fraction over both asserts that they are. **Do not invent the locality check.** Canvas 11's amber row — *"Entrega no Rio de Janeiro/RJ. Veja se você atende essa praça"* — has nothing behind it: `comparablesForItem` has no UF predicate at all, which is why `CLAIMS.md` records *"na sua região"* being removed from two places. Either it is a real read of the edital's delivery clause, produced by the worker like any other finding, or it does not ship. AI prompt or extraction changes run `worker/evaluation` and report the score diff (CLAUDE.md). **Shipped 2026-10-01 in #199, with two of the three states.** `CHECKLIST_KEYS` is ten keys, fixed and ordered; three are answered by PNCP's structured fields before anybody pays and seven need the edital read — which is what a triagem buys, and what this block exists to show. **The denominator does not move**, Sci's ruling against canvas 11's *"3 de 4" → "5 de 7"*: a reader deciding whether to spend a triagem has to be able to see the seven rows that currently say nothing. `Finding.known` was added so an unanswered row cannot be counted as conferida by comparing a value against copy. The checklist ships on `TenderOk`, built **inside the statement that already reads the screening**, so the route pays no extra round trip and the client never receives an analysis it may not have paid for. **`entrega/local` does ship**, and that does not contradict this card: the worker already reads `entrega.local` with a page citation, and what the card warns against — *"na sua região"* — is a claim about the **price band**, whose query has no UF predicate at all. **The `blocker` state does not ship**: see **D35** for the nine live evaluation runs that measured what adding it costs. **There is no prompt change in this PR**, so the score diff to report is that the prompt is untouched. **The review before the PR found four defects with every check green, and two were about what the object says to people.** (1) Nothing kept the reading off the wire: `screeningAvailability` now returns the analysis, so `screening,` instead of the rebuilt `{ready, spent, metered}` ships the whole `ai_analyses.result` to the browser — **legal TypeScript** and green on 1 333 tests, and the route had no test file at all. It has one now, and the mutation reddens it. (2) The checklist was gated on neither `spent` nor the company. **It is now gated on `spent`, not `ready`** — §3.2 shares a reading and §10 allocates it per user, so a tender somebody else paid to read is unread here, and the page numbers in it were bought with their triagem. (3) **`cnae` could not say "checked, and no"**: an empty `matchedSegments` means either that no company was searched or that one was and does not cover this tender, which is the Radar's entire `keyword` group — so every reader arriving from Palavras-chave was told we had not checked a CNAE we had checked and rejected. (4) **`meEpp` was locked to PNCP, which is silent on 15 189 of 47 991 tenders (31.6%)**; where it says nothing the row now falls through to the reading, which does answer it and carries a page. Also: the four `currentAnalysis` subqueries became one lateral (no tiebreaker in that ordering, so in principle two columns could come from different rows; 6 shared buffers instead of 24), four assertions relaxed to `toMatchObject` were restored to `toEqual`, and the docstring claiming *"analysis is null exactly when ready is false"* was wrong — a `no_text` row is `ready` with no reading, and production has one | C1 | | 2 | One checklist object, typed in `contract.ts`, with a fixed key set and provenance per row — **built in the web route rather than in the worker**, because the *before* state has no worker output to be produced from and the route already holds both halves; **two of the three states**, `ok` and `unknown`, with `blocker` declared, unemitted and carded as D35 against a measured evaluation regression; the same object describes a tender before and after a screening, with the unread rows marked `unknown` rather than absent; no row is emitted for a condition nothing reads from the edital; the evaluation score diff is reported |
| D27 | D | **Draw the checklist, and replace the meter rather than fixing it.** Depends on D26's object. Canvas 11's meter falls from *"3 de 4 conferidos"* (75%) to *"5 de 7 conferidos"* (71%) at the moment the user spends a triagem: the denominator grows faster than the numerator, so the better-informed state looks worse. Three defects compound and none is a rendering bug. **It is not progress** — the user does no work between the two states, and a four-segment bar promises a task with an end. **It averages two epistemic classes** — checkable PNCP fields and findings read from a PDF whose own screen admits a 6-of-7 citation rate. **It has no third bucket** for the rows D26 marks `unknown`. **So: counts of exceptions, never a ratio.** Lead with the only number that changes a decision — *"1 exigência pode te barrar"* (with its page link) and *"1 coisa para você conferir"* — then the checked list, then coverage as a plain sourced sentence split by origin and never combined. A count can only rise as the product learns more, which is the behaviour the meter got backwards. **Two blocks, one component, two instances.** *"Por que este edital apareceu para você"* is a claim about the **matcher** — why this row is in your list, from structured fields, permanent. What the triagem finds is a claim about the **edital text** — probabilistic, page-cited, revisable. Mutating one heading between two meanings is how a reader concludes the first answer was wrong; so two headings, discriminated on D26's provenance field, with the PNCP block collapsing to one summary line above the AI block rather than disappearing. **Gate on `spent`, not `ready`.** §3.2 shares an analysis across users, so a tender someone else has read is still a first-time request for this viewer — the same distinction Sci already ruled on for the CTA label. `getScreening()` spends nothing, so reading it on the tender screen is free. The risk verdict on the badge row (*"Difícil para empresa pequena · 4/10"*) rides on the same gate and **must carry its label** — `radar.screening.verdict.scoreLabel` = *"Nota da triagem"* already ships and canvas 11 dropped it; a bare "4 de 10" under a heading reading *"Difícil"* has no polarity, and the scale is 10 = better. Signal the transition by **provenance, not animation**: *"Lido por IA em 29/09"* belongs as the AI block's header, not a footnote. An inline region that changes after a screening needs `aria-live="polite"` and a heading that takes focus — neither assertable under `environment: 'node'`, so Playwright or it is unverified **Blocked on D35 for its own lead sentence, 2026-10-01.** This card's first line is *"1 exigência pode te barrar"* — a count of `blocker` rows — and D26 ships a checklist that can never emit one, because joining a barrier to a row needs a prompt change that failed the evaluation gate. Drawn before D35, the headline reads *"0 exigências podem te barrar"* on every edital in the product: a sentence generated by a gap rather than by a reading, which is the exact shape `CLAIMS.md` exists for. **And the mirror of it:** D26's only numbers are `checked`/`total`, every docstring in it is justified by canvas 11's *"N de M conferidos"*, and this card forbids rendering a ratio. One of the two has to give, and it is a copy decision — see D28(c) on *conferido* sliding into *"I was checked and I passed"*. Measured through the real path over the 27 production readings: **median 8 of 10 after a triagem**, only 5 of 27 reach 10/10, and always 3 of 10 before one. | D26, D35 | | 2 | The block renders in both states from one component and two instances, with two headings and never one mutating heading; no ratio appears anywhere; exceptions lead; `unknown` rows are visible rather than buried; the after state is gated on `spent` and a tender read by somebody else still reads as first-time for this viewer; the score renders with *"Nota da triagem"*; a Playwright spec asserts the live region and the focus move |
| D30 | D | ~~**The Radar list grid asks the window the same question D29 just stopped the tender screen asking.**~~ **Done 2026-09-30 in #190, and corrected in #191 — read the correction, because #190 merged before the review came back.** `radar-view.tsx` is a `@container` wrapper with `@min-[860px]:grid-cols-2 @min-[1080px]:grid-cols-3`, and **both numbers are the old thresholds restated rather than new ones**: the list is `main` (`max-w-[1120px]`) inside `px-gutter`, so it measures `min(viewport − rail, 1120) − 40`; 860 is what that was at the old 900px breakpoint, where no rail exists yet, and 1080 is `main` at its cap — the width the old `min-[1280px]` query actually delivered in the one case it was right, the rail collapsed. Below `lg` the two rules are identical; **three bands move, and they are the three the window could not see**: 1024–1163 with the rail out (2 → **1**, the cards were ~355px), 1280–1383 with the rail out (3 → **2**, ~318px), and **1176–1279 with the rail collapsed (2 → 3)** — that last one the rule working rather than escaping it, since the column there really is 1080px, but it is the only place a card gets *narrower* than before (535 → 353px). The `@container` is on the list and **not** on the enclosing `px-gutter` div, for D29’s reason — `container-type: inline-size` also makes an element a containing block for `fixed` descendants and **D25 (3) adds a fixed bottom bar to these very screens**. **What #191 corrects, and why the sequence matters more than any of it.** #190 was merged while the §4b review was still reading the diff, so `main` carried the first version for a few minutes and a green suite was the only thing that had vouched for it. The review found four things. **(1)** The unit tests pinned almost nothing: they asked whether `@container` appeared *before* the grid in the output string with `<ul` somewhere between — a **substring** relation, not an ancestor one — so `@container` on the `px-gutter` div, on `<main>`, or on a **sibling** of the list all passed, and the first two change no width at all, so they passed the browser suite too. **The one decision this card exists to make was guarded by nothing.** The tests now assert the parent–child relation as markup and all four shapes redden. **(2)** A comment row read *"1024 with the rail collapsed — was one"*; the old viewport rule gave **two**. **(3)** A test comment claimed the old rule *"refused columns where there is room"* with no test at any such width; there is one now, 1200px collapsed, which is where the claim is true. **(4)** A `npx prettier --write` reformatted **1 300 lines** of two files into a quote-and-semicolon style no other file in `apps/web` uses — there is no Prettier config here, so lint never objected, and a 90%-noise diff defeats the review step itself. Reverted; the change is 157 lines. Which test does which, per §4c: the unit tests pin the *mechanism* (the container, **its placement**, both thresholds, and no viewport query in any spelling — `lg:` and `xl:` sailed through the first guard) and `e2e/journeys/radar-columns.spec.ts` pins the *result* at 390, 1023, 1024, 1160, 1180, 1280, 1380 and 1440, plus 1024 and 1200 with the rail collapsed. **No case sits on a threshold**: 1164 is the width at which the column is exactly 860.000px, and a test pinned there asserts only that a tie breaks the way it broke today. Mutation-checked with every restore byte-identical by `md5`: reverting the thresholds reddens 6 of the 10 browser cases and leaves green the 4 where the rules agree; each of the four container placements reddens the unit suite. Nothing for `CLAIMS.md`: no copy, no schema, no new promise. **The card’s claim to be "the only other instance" was wrong** — see **D32**. Original card: **The Radar list grid asks the window the same question D29 just stopped the tender screen asking.** Found 2026-09-30 while writing D29's rule into `CLAUDE.md` — testing the rule against the codebase is what turned it up, and it is the only other instance: every remaining `min-[Npx]:` under `app/radar/` and `app/conta/` is at **560px**, which is safe, because the rail does not exist below `lg` and by 1024px the content box (720px) has already cleared 560. `radar-view.tsx:710` is not: `grid-cols-1 … min-[900px]:grid-cols-2 min-[1280px]:grid-cols-3`, both above the 720px line, on a `main` of `max-w-[1120px]` sitting beside a 264px rail. So two columns fire when the window says 900 while the column has ~760, and three fire at 1280 with ~1016 — cards of ~330px on the screen a user spends the most time on. **Less severe than D29 and the same defect**: the block is sized by the window and drawn in the column. Fix is D29's, done twice: `@container` on a wrapper, `@min-[Npx]:` on the grid, thresholds derived from the content box and the arithmetic written in the comment. **These numbers are read from the source, not measured in a browser** — D29's own lesson is that `renderToStaticMarkup` cannot see a layout, so confirm the widths the way D29 did (`e2e/journeys/tender-columns.spec.ts` is the pattern, and the rail-collapsed case is the one a viewport query can never get right). Not a launch blocker: the product is mobile-first by Sci's 2026-09-29 ruling and this is desktop-only tightness, which is why it sits here and not in M3 | — | ∥ | 0.25 | The Radar list is two or three columns only where each card has room, at every width from 900px to 1440px with the rail expanded and collapsed; the thresholds are container queries with their arithmetic in the comment; and a browser test pins it, because a unit test cannot |
| D32 | D | **The Itens table swaps layouts at a viewport breakpoint too — D30 swept one spelling and missed it.** Found 2026-09-30 by the review of #190, which took D30’s claim to be *"the only other instance"* and checked it. | D33 | D | **Nothing on a tender screen knows which items can be priced, and the flag that was used for it means something else.** Found 2026-09-30 when Sci asked why only two items of an edital had price history and the answer turned out to need a signal that does not exist. D25 (5) opens the preço screen on `items.find(i => i.hasAward)`, and E9's list of items shows them all undifferentiated; Sci's request is to **mark the ones with winners' data and put them first**. `hasAward` cannot do it: it is *this item's own* award, `true` for **0.33%** of items on open editais and **zero** on all six tenders Sci sent, so the ordering has always fallen through to `items[0]` and a marker built on it would mark nothing. The real signal is *"does `priceBand(comparablesForItem(...))` return non-null"*, and that is **412 ms median and 2 715 ms worst per item** (B21) — fifty-one items is not a page render. So it is a **precomputed per-item flag written by a job**, which belongs with B21's index and caching decision, and it is **worth nothing until C3**: after the product-identity gate the honest count today is ~0 priceable items, so the column would be empty on every screen. Sequence it after C3, and do not ship a marker before then — an empty column is the same defect as a wrong number, one shade quieter | C3, B21 | ∥ | 0.5 | Each item in the preço screen's list says whether a band exists for it, from a stored flag and not from a query per item; the screen opens on an item that has one; and when none has one the screen says so once instead of leaving the reader to click fifty-one times. A test proves the flag and the band agree, because a flag that disagrees with the screen is worse than no flag |
| D34 | D | **A 251-item list is not a list, and the two useful rows are somewhere in it.** **The *arrangement* half is done — 2026-10-05, `task/d34-item-list`; the account is at the end of this card. The *ordering* half is still open and still blocked on D33 + C3, and the hook is in the code, inert and tested as inert.** Sci, 2026-10-01, on `77817476000144-1-000034/2026`: *"imagine the tender has more than 31 items… if the item has this range price, it should be visible in priority, if not, ok. But we need a new arrangement to view those items, expand/collapse. But scrolling 31 items is not the best practice."* The preço screen lists every item of the edital as equal rows — 251 on that tender, 91 on FIOCRUZ's — and the only way to find the ones with a price range is to open each one. **Two halves, and they ship independently.** The *arrangement* needs nothing new: a collapsed list that opens, a count, and a way to search within it, so a long edital stops being a scroll. The *priority* half — winner-bearing items first, marked — needs the per-item flag **D33** builds, and is worth nothing before **C3**, because after the product-identity gate the honest count of priceable items is ~0. **Ship the arrangement now and the ordering when the signal exists**; do not fake the ordering with `hasAward`, which is the item's *own* award and is `false` on every open edital — that mistake is already recorded on D25 (5) and D33. Note the product framing this came with, because it is the point of the whole screen: a past tender's winning prices are the baseline a newcomer reads a new tender against, and this list is where that baseline is found or lost. **What shipped, 2026-10-05 (the arrangement).** `item-picker.tsx`: above **8** items the chip list becomes a `<details>` — `tender-items.tsx`’s idiom, not a fourth shape, and D39 is still the card that extracts them — carrying the count (`radar.opportunity.items.showing`, reused), a search field, and **the chosen item inside the `<summary>`, which is what survives the collapse**. At or below 8 it is the list this screen already had, by `LongText`’s rule that a short thing gets no disclosure. The open list is bounded (`max-h-[60vh]`, scrolls inside itself) and capped at **50 rows**, because the widest tender we hold has 1 124 items; past the cap the search is the way through rather than a *Ver mais*, since paging **and** a filter together would hide a match behind a button and make the count mean two things. Search folds accents and case, requires every token, and includes the item **number** — on a 251-item edital the number is often all the reader has, the description having been cut at 70. **No new copy**: `itemsLabel`, `item`, `items.showMore`/`.less`/`.showing` already say these things; the search field therefore has **no words of its own** and borrows the group heading through `aria-labelledby`, which is **D47**. **No width breakpoint at all** — the summary row wraps — because this block is in `main` (`max-w-[960px] px-gutter`) beside the 264px rail, so its column is `min(viewport − rail, 960) − 40` = 720px at 1024px, and a number nobody has measured is D32’s mistake; a test fails if a viewport query ever appears here, in either spelling. **What the §4b review changed, before the PR opened.** The first version capped the open list with a plain `matched.slice(0, 50)`, and **both suites chose item 1**, so nothing could see that opening the 251-item edital on `?item=251` drew fifty rows without it — the summary chip is hidden while the list is open, so `aria-current` was then **nowhere on the screen**, against this card’s own criterion. `rowsToDraw` moves the chosen row to the front of the matches; a 251-item browser case and a unit case pin it. Four more: `vh` → `dvh` (on mobile `vh` is the viewport with the URL bar retracted — and the test guarding it would have **reddened on the fix**); NFD → **NFKD**, so `g/m2`, `no 12` and `3a` find `g/m²`, `nº 12` and `3ª`, which NFD leaves untouched; the viewport tripwire rewritten token-by-token after the review showed the regex missed `max-[900px]:` and `[@media(…)]:` while flagging the legitimate `@max-md:`; and `min-w-0 break-words` on the chip label, `EvidenceRow`’s rule, without which a 70-character code pushes the chip past its column. The reachability test in `price-view.test.tsx` asked for `<summary` anywhere on the page — a substring relation wearing an ancestor relation’s clothes, #190’s defect — and now names the picker. **Split per §4c and mutation-proven both ways**: removing the filter leaves every vitest case **green** and reddens 3 of 10 browser cases — the clearest demonstration in the repo of what `environment: 'node'` cannot see | — | ∥ | 0.5 | A 251-item edital is navigable without scrolling it: the list collapses to a readable height, says how many items it holds, and opens on demand; the chosen item stays visible when it does; and the ordering hook is present but inert until D33 supplies the flag, with a test that proves it is inert rather than wrong |

Sci ruled 2026-10-01 that a row is barred **only** when the reading says so — a row that merely demands something is *answered*, not barred, because the fraction counts *conferidos* and not *passed* — and `bloqueadores_pequena_empresa` is `[{ponto, pagina}]`, free text joinable to no row. **Measured, nine live `python -m evaluation` runs on 2026-10-01, same model and session, R$ 0,07 total:** untouched the prompt scored **96,6 / 96,6 / 98,3** and never missed the 95% gate; with a `chave` enum inside the JSON skeleton **94,8 / 96,6 / 93,1** (2 of 3 below the gate); with a lighter `chave: null` plus short tokens in the instruction **81,0 / 96,6 / 96,6**. The baseline's *worst* run beats either variant's mean, so neither shipped. Two failures are constant in every run and are model drift rather than ours — *Sem amostra → nao_informado* and *ME/EPP: tratamento favorecido → sem_beneficio*. **Do not join a barrier to a row by its cited page**: several rows routinely cite the same page, so it would be a guess wearing a citation. What this needs is prompt work measured against the gate rather than asserted — more samples per variant than three, since the spread is wide, and a variant that does not lengthen the JSON skeleton the model reads as its schema. `checklist.test.ts` carries a **tripwire**: it asserts no row is ever `blocker`, so the gap cannot quietly become *"no edital has barriers"*. JSON numbers have no leading zeros, so for any UASG whose code begins with `0` the first digit is gone **before the worker ever sees the value**, and `str()` cannot put back what the wire dropped.
